- Keep Tar Sap keys unavailable to Sycamore, relays, gateways, foreign Trees, and The Forest Canopy
- Remain active until the user or an approved recovery policy confirms that protection has been restored

A compromised or malfunctioning Sycamore should still be unable to decrypt Tar Sap because Tar Sap keys remain bound to the user's local Forest and outside Sycamore's possession.

A Tar Sap emergency notification should state one of the following:

```text
Emergency: A remote user or Forest attempted to Tap your Sycamore Tree.
Emergency: A session attempted to disguise a Tar Sap request.
Emergency: Sycamore can no longer verify Tar Sap protection.
Tar Sap Lockdown is active. Affected remote Paths have been closed.
```

Sycamore must not reveal Tar Sap contents in an emergency message, incident Log, rejection response, or notification sent to the requesting party.

Sycamore's elevated permissions make Sycamore the connection gate and connection manager, not the owner of the user's Forest, network, devices, or Soil.

The user holds final authority.

### Forest Commands

```text
Sycamore, show my connection options.
Sycamore, connect through the Canopy-Headscale qube.
Sycamore, use the best approved connection.
Sycamore, keep this connection local.
Sycamore, use a direct connection when available.
Sycamore, use an approved relay if needed.
Sycamore, enable encrypted internet access.
Sycamore, show the active connection Path.
Sycamore, explain why the connection changed.
```

Sycamore must honor every Sap Tap and Tar Tap lock before routing a Fluid.

A Tar Sap Tap Attempt is an attempt to force access through a Tar Tap without authorization.

A Tar Sap Tap Attempt must trigger the existing Tar Sap emergency and lockdown process.

Sycamore does not replace local Mycelium.

Mycelium handles direct local coordination inside a Forest.

Sycamore handles identity, trust, routing, permissions, sessions, and revocation when a connection reaches another device, network, user, or Forest.

Cross-Forest Mycelium remains disabled unless an approved Sycamore bridge explicitly supports it.

### Sycamore and the Canopy

Sycamore governs Canopy Links that cross a device, user, network, or Forest boundary.

Sycamore provides:

- Forest and Tree identity
- Trust relationships
- Permission negotiation
- Session creation
- Connection routing
- Relay selection
- Revocation
- Endpoint verification
- Cross-Forest policy enforcement

A Personal Canopy may operate locally without using the wider Forest Canopy.

A connection through the wider Forest Canopy requires Sycamore or an equivalent approved connection guardian.

Sycamore does not decide Maple's automation logic and does not modify the Grain Patterns or Leaves of participating Trees.

### Sycamore and Sap Taps

Sycamore interacts with Sap Taps as the connection, endpoint, route, session, and cross-Forest enforcement Tree.

Sycamore may:

- Verify sending and receiving identities
- Request route-scoped access from the Spirit
- Open or maintain an approved Stream after Tap authorization
- Enforce Water, Sap, and Tar Sap route boundaries
- Carry authorized Sap between verified endpoints
- Carry Shared Moss when the user permits synchronization
- Block Tar Moss from cross-Forest synchronization
- Block Tar Sap from leaving the local Forest
- Pause or terminate a route when a Tap locks
- Revoke a session when the Spirit expires its capability
- Preserve origin, final destination, route class, and Action Receipt
- Ask Cedar to inspect suspicious incoming material
- Report route failure or a Tar Sap Tap Attempt to the Spirit

Sycamore must not:

- Own the user's Tap settings
- Select a deeper Fluid absorption mode for a receiving Tree
- Read or absorb routed Sap merely because Sycamore carries it
- Decrypt Tar Sap
- Route Tar Sap or Tar Moss outside the local Forest
- Reopen a locked Tap
- Expand a Moss synchronization scope
- Convert a local-only session into a cross-Forest session without approval
- Weaken the user's classification or absorption rules

The user controls routing permission and absorption settings separately.

Sycamore carries only what the Spirit's Sap Taps permits.

### Sycamore Sap Taps Commands

```text
Sycamore, show which Tap authorizes this route.
Sycamore, route this Sap to the approved endpoint.
Sycamore, stop this route when the Tap locks.
Sycamore, block Tar Moss synchronization.
Sycamore, show Shared Moss destinations.
Sycamore, revoke this cross-Forest session.
Sycamore, report this Tar Sap Tap Attempt.
Sycamore, show origin and final destination.
```

## 🪵 Logs

Logs are durable, long-term data and information that the user or Trees do not want to forget.

Logs may preserve:

- Activity history
- Decisions
- Corrections
- System events
- Conversations selected for retention
- Maintenance history
- Errors
- Backups
- Recovery information
- Important observations
- Long-term memories
- Audit information

Logs should be searchable, dated, attributable, and protected according to their sensitivity.

A Grove Log records Branch Calls, participating Trees, step states, Fluid transfers, permissions, retries, fallbacks, failures, and Action Receipts.

A Grove Log should preserve which Tree performed each part of the task.

## 🏡 Log Cabin

A Log Cabin is the place where Trees and users store Logs, files, backups, snapshots, archives, and long-term data.

Examples include:

- NAS storage
- File managers
- HDDs
- SSDs
- Backup servers
- Archive drives
- Storage pools
- Encrypted vaults
- Remote backup locations

The Log Cabin is not Bark.

Bark is how a user interacts with a Tree.

The Log Cabin is where durable material is stored and protected.

## 🌿 Canopy

The Canopy is the Forest's open branch-and-leaf connection layer.

The Canopy is the second non-Fluid method by which Trees may communicate.

Mycelium connects Trees below the user through Roots, Soil, protected Paths, and code.

The Canopy connects Trees above the user through Branches, Leaves, visible relationships, and open connection points.

The Canopy itself is non-Fluid communication.

The Canopy is part of Tree and Forest connection anatomy and is not Solid Material.

The Canopy may also carry:

- Canopy Passes
- Fluids
- Whole Solid Materials
- Pieces of Solid Materials
- Protected references to Solid Materials

### Personal Canopy

A Personal Canopy is the Canopy belonging to one user's local Forest.

A Personal Canopy links Trees through their approved Branches and Leaves without requiring either Tree to absorb the other's capabilities, complete Leaves, or Grain Pattern.

A Personal Canopy is similar to dragging and dropping information across connected folders.

The connection is direct and easy to use, but it does not preserve a detailed record of every intermediate Branch the information crossed.

A Personal Canopy may support:

- Instant Tree-to-Tree messages
- Branch calls
- Leaf references
- Automation triggers
- Shared task state
- Synchronized actions
- Shared application events
- File viewing, access, copying, or movement through approved Branches
- Solid Material viewing and interaction
- Grove coordination
- Health, availability, and warning signals

### The Forest Canopy

The Forest Canopy is the wider network formed when authorized Personal Canopies connect across devices, users, or Forests.

The Forest Canopy is closer to the internet analogy.

It allows Forests to discover, reach, and cooperate with other authorized Forests without merging their Roots, Leaves, or Grain Patterns.

The Forest Canopy may provide:

- Identity and discovery
- Trusted Forest relationships
- Connection negotiation
- Availability and presence
- Automation discovery
- Cross-Forest event delivery
- Relay selection
- Revocation
- Health and warning distribution
- Access to capabilities available through a connected Forest
- Access to authorized Solid Materials exposed by a connected Forest

Sycamore governs the trust, permissions, sessions, routing, and revocation needed for Personal Canopies to connect through the wider Forest Canopy.

### Canopy Links

A Canopy Link is an authorized Branch-to-Branch or Leaf-to-Leaf connection.

A Canopy Link may be:

- Temporary
- Session-based
- Scheduled
- Reusable
- Medium-term
- Long-term
- Local
- Cross-device
- Cross-Forest

The Canopy is optimized for medium- to long-term linking between two Forests.

A Canopy Link may let one Forest:

- Synchronize approved automations
- Trigger an automation in the connected Forest
- Ask a connected Tree to use a Branch the local Forest does not have
- Share task state
- Coordinate schedules
- Pass approved Leaves, Solid Materials, or Fluids
- View or use a remote file without absorbing its contents
- Use a remote application surface
- Use a remote capability without copying that capability locally

A Canopy Link does not change a participating Tree's Grain Pattern.

A Canopy Link does not automatically add the connected Forest's Leaves, Branches, memories, preferences, Solid Materials, or capabilities to the local Trees.

### Canopy Sections

A Canopy Section is an authorized area exposed through a Canopy Link.

A Canopy Section may represent:

- A Tree
- A Branch or Twig
- A project
- A folder
- A file collection
- An application
- A website or web application
- A media library
- An automation collection
- A shared workspace
- Another approved Forest area

A Tree or user may enter a Canopy Section, view what it exposes, work with approved items, and leave without planting, absorbing, or growing copies of the exposed information.

### Canopy Communication

Canopy communication moves openly from connecting Branch to connecting Branch.

The Canopy is as fast as Mycelium for ordinary authorized communication.

The major difference is evidence and memory.

Mycelium preserves protected Paths and normally creates Mycelial Trace Leaves.

The Canopy does not normally save the route taken from Branch to Branch.

The Canopy does not normally generate content Leaves merely because information or Solid Material passed through it.

Trees attached to a Canopy usually remember no more than a minimal connection fact such as:

```text
I was connected to this Canopy.
```

A Canopy connection record may include:

- Canopy identifier
- Connected Forest or Tree
- Start and end time
- Permission profile
- Origin
- Final destination
- Success, failure, or revocation state
- Action Receipt identifier

It should not include every Branch hop unless a specific diagnostic or security policy temporarily enables route tracing.

### Canopy Pass

A Canopy Pass is a direct non-Fluid communication event carried through a Canopy Link.

A Canopy Pass may carry:

- An event
- A trigger
- A Branch request
- A Leaf reference
- A status
- A capability request
- A synchronization signal
- A task checkpoint
- A minimal result
- A request to open or interact with Solid Material

A Canopy Pass does not require the receiving Tree to absorb the information as a Leaf.

The receiving Tree may act on the event and allow it to pass without changing its Leaves, Growth Rings, preferences, or Grain Pattern.

### Canopy Payloads

A Canopy Payload is something made available through a Canopy Link.

A Canopy Payload may be:

```text
Canopy Pass
Fluid
Whole Solid Material
Piece of Solid Material
Protected Solid-Material Reference
```

A Canopy Pass is direct non-Fluid coordination.

A Fluid is transferable information designed to be washed over, sipped, drunk, downloaded, or otherwise absorbed according to permission.

Solid Material retains its own bounded structure and identity.

A Tree or user may look at, open, browse, render, search, or interact with authorized Solid Material while connected to the Canopy without absorbing it.

### Solid Material Through the Canopy

The Canopy may expose, pass, copy, or move whole Solid Materials or selected Pieces of Solid Material.

Examples include:

- Leaves
- Buds
- Blossoms
- Flowers
- Fruit
- Files
- Folders
- Documents
- Images
- Audio
- Video
- Project artifacts
- Website surfaces
- Application surfaces
- Other bounded Forest objects

A Solid Material object may be used in place while it remains in the source Forest.

This resembles a user opening a website, application, shared folder, or remote file without adding the complete source to the user's own Tree.

Accessing Solid Material does not automatically:

- Plant it
- Absorb it
- Add it to Leaf Foliage
- Grow new content Leaves
- Add it to memory
- Change a Grain Pattern
- Copy it into local Soil
- Give the receiving Tree ownership
- Permit external resharing

A Tree may hold temporary **Canopy View Context** while rendering or interacting with Solid Material.

Canopy View Context is session state rather than a Leaf.

It normally disappears when the Canopy session ends.

### Canopy Material Modes

Solid Material access through the Canopy may use separately authorized modes:

- **View** — render or inspect without changing or copying
- **Interact** — use the source application, website, file, or object in place
- **Reference** — retain a protected pointer without copying the content
- **Copy Piece** — copy an approved portion
- **Copy Whole** — copy the complete approved object
- **Move** — transfer the approved object to another location
- **Edit in Place** — change the source object through its owning Tree or application
- **Download** — place a copy into destination Soil without automatically absorbing it into a Tree

Viewing, interacting, referencing, copying to Soil, and absorbing into a Tree are separate actions.

A downloaded file may exist in Soil without becoming part of a Tree's Leaf Foliage.

When a Tree actually incorporates the contents into its decisions, memory, growth, preferences, or internal knowledge, absorption rules apply.

### Leaves and Fluids Through the Canopy

Leaves, other Solid Materials, and Fluids may pass through the Canopy.

For ordinary Canopy transit, they retain:

- Origin location
- Final destination
- Object or Fluid identity
- Protection classification
- Permission scope
- Delivery result

They do not normally retain the complete intermediate route.

A Fluid remains a Fluid while traveling through the Canopy.

A Solid Material remains Solid Material unless an authorized action copies, changes, converts, or absorbs it.

A Leaf may be exposed whole or in part as Solid Material.

A Leaf reference remains a reference unless the receiving Tree chooses and is permitted to absorb the represented information.

The Canopy should not silently grow new content Leaves in intermediate or receiving Trees.

### Moss in the Canopy

Moss is cookie-like Solid Material used by the Canopy to remember settings, preferences, connection state, and resume information.

Moss may cling to:

- A Canopy
- A Canopy Section
- A Tree
- A Branch or Twig
- A Leaf or Solid Material reference
- A website
- A web application
- A local or remote application
- A folder
- A project
- An automation workspace

Moss may remember:

- Canopy identifier
- Connected Tree or Forest
- Canopy Section
- Branch, website, or application last used
- Last open item or protected reference
- Last position, page, panel, tab, folder, or workspace
- Website settings
- Website preferences
- Application settings
- Application preferences
- Theme
- Language
- Zoom
- Layout
- Panel arrangement
- Filters
- Sort order
- Display preferences
- Interaction preferences
- Notification preferences
- Recently used tools or sections
- Task or automation checkpoints
- Permission-profile references
- Expiration
- Resume state

Moss is Solid Material rather than a Leaf.

Moss does not enter Leaf Foliage merely because it exists.

A Tree may intentionally absorb approved Moss and grow a Leaf from it, but that is a separate action.

The complete Moss definition appears in the dedicated Moss section.


### Canopy Sources and Tree Input Leaves

Viewing a website, application, file, video, Leaf, Flower, or other Solid Material through the Canopy does not automatically create a Tree Input Leaf.

A Tree Input Leaf is created when the Tree intentionally:

- Saves source information
- Logs source information
- Indexes source information
- Summarizes source information
- Absorbs source information
- Retains source details for future work

Moss remains settings, preference, connection, and resume Solid Material rather than a Tree Input Leaf containing the source material.

The source medium does not determine the category.

The source's provenance and the Tree's retention action determine the category.

### Canopy Automation

The Canopy is one of Maple's primary automation networks.

Maple should use the Canopy when an automation requires:

- A reusable link between Trees
- A medium- or long-term link between Forests
- Cross-device coordination
- Cross-Forest automation synchronization
- An automation capability that exists only in a connected Forest
- Fast event delivery without storing detailed Paths
- Triggering a remote Branch without copying it
- Viewing or interacting with connected Solid Material
- Accessing remote files or applications without absorbing them
- Minimal effect on local Leaves and Grain Patterns

Maple may coordinate a Grove through the Canopy while keeping participating Trees unchanged.

Maple may trigger an automation in a connected Forest even when the local Trees do not have that automation's Branches.

Maple should use Mycelium when deep local access, Soil access, Root coordination, detailed Paths, or Mycelial Trace Leaves are useful.

Maple should use Fluids when information must be explicitly absorbed or delivered as transferable meaning.

Maple should use Solid Material access when the user or Tree needs to view, interact with, copy, move, or download a bounded object without automatically absorbing it.

### Canopy, Mycelium, Solid Material, and Fluids

```text
Mycelium
= Root-level connection below the user
= Deep local access, Paths, Soil, code, and Trace Leaves

Personal Canopy
= Branch-and-Leaf connection above the user
= Drag-and-drop-style linking inside or between approved Forest spaces

The Forest Canopy
= Wider network of connected Forest Canopies
= Internet-like discovery, reachability, and cross-Forest linking

Solid Material
= Whole bounded objects or selected pieces
= May be viewed or accessed without being absorbed

Fluids
= Eye-level transferable meaning
= Designed for explicit delivery, management, and possible absorption
```

### Canopy Health and Awareness

The Canopy also remains the shared visibility, health-awareness, and security-warning layer formed across connected Trees and Forests.

It may carry:

- Signed health reports
- Availability and reachability
- Integrity and version status
- Damage reports
- Mold indicators
- Hacker indicators
- Security alerts
- Quarantine notices
- Recovery status
- Service health
- Sapling status
- Cedar reports
- User-visible warnings

### Canopy Health States

- **Healthy** — operating normally with valid recent reports
- **Watch** — unusual behavior or a condition requiring attention
- **Wounded** — confirmed damage, compromise, or serious failure
- **Quarantined** — intentionally isolated to protect the Forest
- **Dormant** — intentionally inactive or offline
- **Unknown** — no valid recent health report

### Canopy Security Rules

The Canopy should:

- Use verified Tree and Forest identities
- Enforce Branch, Leaf, Solid Material, automation, user, device, and Forest permissions
- Separate view, interact, reference, copy, move, edit, download, absorb, execute, and share permissions
- Share the minimum information required
- Preserve privacy boundaries
- Keep origin and final-destination receipts
- Use Sycamore for cross-Forest trust, routing, and revocation
- Allow immediate disconnection
- Distinguish a Canopy Pass, Fluid transfer, and Solid Material access
- Prevent silent Grain Pattern or Leaf changes
- Treat suspicious or unauthorized Canopy Links as a security event
- Permit temporary diagnostic route tracing only under an approved policy

The Canopy is not permission to read every Tree memory, Log, file, conversation, Root, application, or complete Leaf Foliage.

The absence of a full hop-by-hop route means Canopy security depends strongly on endpoint identity, permissions, integrity, and Action Receipts.

### Canopy Commands

```text
Check the Canopy.
Show my Personal Canopy.
Show the wider Forest Canopy.
Connect these Trees through my Canopy.
Link these Forests through their Canopies.
Show active Canopy Links.
Show Canopy Sections.
Open this Solid Material through the Canopy.
View this without absorbing it.
Interact with this application through the Canopy.
Copy this Piece of Solid Material.
Download this file without absorbing it.
Show what passed through this Canopy.
Show the origin and final destination.
Grow Moss for this Canopy Section.
Show Moss.
Scrape off this Moss.
Resume from this Moss.
Disconnect this Canopy Link.
Revoke this Forest from my Canopy.
Maple, use the Canopy for this automation.
Maple, synchronize this automation with the connected Forest.
Use Mycelium instead.
Use a Fluid instead.
Patrol the Canopy.
Raise an alarm through the Canopy.
Show Moss for this website.
Show Moss for this application.
Grow Moss for this connection.
Remove cookies.
Reject Moss growth for this website.
Reject Moss growth for this application.
Allow Moss growth here.
Oil this Moss.
Tar this Moss.
Oil and tar this Moss.
```

## 🪵 Solid Material

Solid Material is bounded non-Fluid data or a bounded non-Fluid capability object that is not the base Tree itself.

Solid Material preserves its own identity, structure, source, provenance, protection, and lifecycle while it is viewed, accessed, exposed, copied, moved, installed, detached, recycled, or downloaded.

Solid Material may be whole or partial.

A **Piece of Solid Material** is an authorized portion of a larger Solid Material object.

Examples of Solid Material include:

- Leaves
- Buds
- Blossoms
- Flowers
- Fruit
- Moss
- Twigs
- Leaf Litter
- Fertilizer Files
- Seeds and Propagations when represented as bounded packages
- Files
- Folders
- Documents
- Images
- Audio
- Video
- Project artifacts
- Website surfaces
- Application surfaces
- Other bounded non-Fluid data or capability objects

The base Tree as a whole is not Solid Material.

An active Potted Tree or booted Cherry Bonsai is also a Tree rather than Solid Material.

Its stored Boot Package, Bonsai package, Propagule, Propagation, or recovery archive is Solid Material.

The Tree may contain, grow, use, expose, detach, copy, recycle, or absorb Solid Material without becoming Solid Material itself.

A Twig is Solid Material because it is a bounded capability object, even while it is attached to a Branch.

Invoking a Twig does not automatically copy or absorb the Twig.

### What Is Not Solid Material

The following are not Solid Material:

- The base Tree as a whole
- The Canopy
- Mycelium

The Canopy and Mycelium are considered connection and communication parts of Trees and the Forest.

They may carry, expose, locate, or provide access to Solid Material.

They are not themselves Solid Material.

A Fluid is also not Solid Material while it remains a Fluid.

Solid Material differs from a Fluid.

```text
Solid Material
= bounded non-Fluid data or capability object

Fluid
= transferable meaning designed for delivery and possible absorption

Tree
= the active entity that grows, uses, protects, and coordinates material

Canopy and Mycelium
= Tree and Forest connection systems
```

A user or Tree may view or interact with Solid Material through an active Canopy Link without planting it, copying it into local Soil, or growing new Leaves from it.

A Tree may use temporary Canopy View Context to render or operate the source.

Canopy View Context is not ordinary Leaf Foliage and expires with the session unless Moss is authorized to preserve settings, preferences, location, or resume state.

### Solid Material Conversion

Solid Material may be converted into a Fluid when approved information must be absorbed, summarized, routed as transferable meaning, or retained independently by a Tree.

A Fluid may be converted into Solid Material when its information is formed into a bounded object such as:

- A Leaf
- A note
- A Bud
- A Blossom
- A Flower
- A Fruit
- A file
- A document
- A media object
- Another approved artifact

Conversion should preserve provenance, protection, user scope, source identity, and the difference between copied content and a protected reference.

### Solid Material Commands

```text
Show this Solid Material.
Show the whole object.
Show only this Piece.
View this through the Canopy without absorbing it.
Copy this Piece into Soil.
Convert this Solid Material into a Fluid.
Convert this Fluid into Solid Material.
Show whether this was viewed, copied, downloaded, or absorbed.
```

### Solid Material and Tree Input Leaves

External Solid Material becomes a Tree Input Leaf only when a Tree saves, logs, indexes, summarizes, or absorbs information from it.

Merely viewing or interacting with the Solid Material through the Canopy does not create a Tree Input Leaf.

A user-provided Solid Material object is classified through User Input provenance instead.

### Moss as Solid Material

Moss is small Solid Material used for cookie-like settings, preferences, connection state, and resumption.

Moss may be viewed, copied, synchronized, protected, cleared, or revoked without entering Leaf Foliage.

A Tree grows a Leaf from Moss only when it intentionally absorbs, learns from, summarizes, or permanently remembers approved Moss information.

## 🟩 Moss

Moss is small cookie-like Solid Material that clings to Canopies, Branches, Leaves, websites, applications, projects, and other Canopy Sections.

Moss helps a user or Tree return to a connected place and continue from where they stopped.

Moss is not an ordinary Leaf.

Moss does not enter Leaf Foliage merely because it exists.

Moss may be viewed, passed through the Canopy, copied, synchronized, protected, cleared, expired, or revoked as Solid Material.

### What Moss Remembers

Moss may remember the minimum information needed to restore an approved experience, including:

- Canopy and Canopy Section identifiers
- Connected Tree, Forest, Branch, or Twig
- Website or application identity
- Last page, file, panel, tab, folder, or workspace
- Last open Solid Material reference
- Scroll position
- Playback position
- Cursor position
- Website settings
- Website preferences
- Application settings
- Application preferences
- Theme
- Language
- Zoom
- Layout
- Panel arrangement
- Filters
- Sort order
- Display preferences
- Interaction preferences
- Notification preferences
- Recently used tools or sections
- Task or automation checkpoints
- Permission-profile references
- Session identifiers
- Expiration
- Resume state

Moss should not copy the complete website, application, file, Leaf, or other Solid Material merely to remember where the user stopped.

### Moss and Cookies

Moss behaves similarly to website and application cookies.

Like cookies, Moss may help a connected website or application remember:

- That the user or Tree visited before
- Which approved settings were selected
- Which preferences apply to that website or application
- Which section was last open
- How the interface should appear
- Which approved session or workspace should resume

Forest Moss must remain more visible and controllable than ordinary hidden tracking cookies.

Moss must be:

- User-visible
- Scope-limited
- Permission-controlled
- Clearable
- Revocable
- Expiring when appropriate
- Separated from raw credentials and private keys
- Prevented from silently tracking unrelated Canopies, websites, applications, users, or Forests

### Moss Types

- **Session Moss** lasts only for an active Canopy, website, application, or task session
- **Persistent Moss** remains for an approved reusable connection or saved experience
- **Shared Moss** synchronizes approved settings or resume state between authorized Trees or Forests
- **Protected Moss** contains sensitive settings or references and uses the required protection
- **Tar Moss** is Moss protected at Tar Sap grade and kept inside the user's local Forest

Persistent or Shared Moss does not automatically become Tree memory.

Tar Moss cannot pass through the wider Forest Canopy, synchronize to another Forest, or be read by an unauthorized Tree.

### Growing Moss

A website, application, Tree, Branch, or Canopy Section may request permission to grow Moss.

Moss growth may be allowed:

- Once
- For the current session
- For one website
- For one application
- For one Canopy Section
- For one project
- For one Tree
- For an approved reusable connection

The user may reject Moss growth.

Rejecting Moss growth prevents new Moss from being created in the selected scope.

Rejecting growth does not automatically remove existing Moss.

Existing Moss remains until it expires, is revoked, or is scraped off.

A Tree should reject or pause Moss growth when the request is suspicious, overly broad, unrelated to the current activity, or attempts hidden tracking.

### Scraping Off Moss

Scraping off Moss removes the selected Moss object.

```text
Scrape off this Moss.
Remove cookies.
```

`Remove cookies.` is the plain-language alias for scraping Moss.

When no wider scope is named, `Remove cookies.` applies to the current website, application, or Canopy Section.

The user may also scrape:

- One Moss object
- All Moss from a website
- All Moss from an application
- All Moss from a Canopy Section
- All Moss from a connected Forest
- All non-protected Moss
- All Moss after reviewing protected items

Scraping Moss does not delete the source website, application, Leaf, file, project, or other Solid Material.

### Oiling Moss

Oiling Moss is a non-destructive Cedar review of the Moss object.

Cedar Oil may inspect Moss for:

- Hidden trackers
- Unexpected cross-site or cross-Canopy identifiers
- Suspicious permission references
- Malformed or executable content
- Unknown destinations
- Unnecessary source data
- Credential material
- Tampering
- Mold indicators
- Scope expansion

```text
Oil this Moss.
```

Oiling does not delete the Moss or automatically change its settings.

When Cedar is absent, the Moss may be marked `Awaiting Cedar Oil`.

Maple may perform a local safeguard review, but a Maple review must not be labeled Cedar Oiled.

### Tarring Moss

Tarring Moss applies Tar Sap-grade protection to the Moss.

```text
Tar this Moss.
```

Tar Moss:

- Remains local to the user's Forest
- Cannot synchronize through the wider Forest Canopy
- Cannot be shared with another Forest
- Uses local protected storage
- Requires purpose-specific access
- Keeps its original website, application, and Canopy scope
- Does not automatically become Molasses
- Does not automatically enter Leaf Foliage

The user may request both operations:

```text
Oil and tar this Moss.
```

The Forest should Oil the Moss first when Cedar is available, then apply Tar Sap protection.

When Cedar is absent, the Forest may apply Tar protection and mark the Oil review as pending.

### Moss and Leaves

Moss normally prevents Trees from needing to grow Leaves merely to remember website settings, application preferences, Canopy locations, or resume state.

A Tree may create a Leaf from Moss only when it intentionally absorbs or learns from the Moss.

Examples include:

- Turning a repeated application setting into a user-approved preference
- Remembering a workflow checkpoint outside the Canopy
- Learning an approved interface preference
- Saving a website setting as a project rule

The resulting Leaf follows the normal User Input, User Output, Tree Input, or Tree Output provenance rules.

Moss itself remains Solid Material.

A site-specific or application-specific Moss preference must not silently become a global Grain Pattern preference.

### User Moss Authority

The user manages Moss through the Spirit's Sap Taps Moss Gate.

The user controls:

- Whether Moss may grow
- Which scope may grow it
- Which settings and preferences it may remember
- Whether it is Session, Persistent, Shared, Protected, or Tar Moss
- Whether it may synchronize
- Which Trees, websites, applications, devices, users, or Forests may read it
- Whether Cedar Oil is required
- Whether it may be absorbed into a Leaf
- When it expires
- When it is scraped, revoked, or Tared

Maple may manage Moss only within the user's policy.

Cedar may Oil or quarantine Moss.

Sycamore may synchronize only approved Shared Moss.

The Spirit applies and enforces the user's Moss policy.

### Moss Security

Moss must not contain:

- Raw passwords
- Private keys
- Recovery codes
- Complete viewed Solid Material
- Unnecessary source content
- A complete intermediate Canopy route
- Hidden permission expansion
- Unapproved cross-site tracking identifiers
- Unapproved cross-Canopy tracking identifiers

Moss should preserve:

- Owner
- Scope
- Source website, application, Branch, or Canopy Section
- Creation time
- Last use
- Expiration
- Protection level
- Permission profile
- Sync destinations
- Oil status
- Tar status
- Clear and revoke controls

A website, application, Tree, or Forest must not read unrelated Moss without permission.

### Moss Commands

```text
Show Moss.
Show Moss for this website.
Show Moss for this application.
Show Moss attached to this Canopy Section.
Show what settings this Moss remembers.
Grow Moss for this connection.
Keep this Moss between sessions.
Make this Session Moss.
Make this Persistent Moss.
Reject Moss growth.
Reject Moss growth for this website.
Reject Moss growth for this application.
Allow Moss growth here.
Scrape off this Moss.
Remove cookies.
Clear all Moss for this website.
Clear all Moss for this application.
Revoke this Shared Moss.
Oil this Moss.
Tar this Moss.
Oil and tar this Moss.
Show the Oil status of this Moss.
Show the Tar status of this Moss.
Resume from this Moss.
Do not let this Moss affect other Canopy Sections.
Absorb this Moss into a Leaf.
```

## 🌊 Streams

Streams are sustained Paths that carry a consistent flow of Fluid between authorized Trees, Forest components, applications, devices, or users.

A Stream is a delivery pattern rather than a Fluid class.

A Stream may carry:

- Water
- Sap
- Syrup
- A locally authorized Tar Sap flow that never leaves the user's Forest
- Files and download chunks
- Screen, audio, video, controller, and game-session state
- Synchronization timing
- Repeated task state
- Approved control messages
- Other authorized Fluid representations

Streams are useful when Trees need a stable connection for longer than a single message or update.

Streams are closer to traditional downloads and live synchronized connections than Rain.

A Stream may support:

- Downloading a file or package
- Watching a movie in sync with a friend
- Screen sharing
- Remote-control sessions
- Voice and video communication
- Shared game sessions
- Repeated file synchronization
- Long-running collaboration
- Continuous device or application status

A Stream lets participating Trees pass relevant information without repeatedly opening a new Tap, sending separate Rain events, or asking every Tree to store and retransmit every individual update.

### Live Streams

A Live Stream is a sustained flow that Trees process primarily in the moment.

Examples include synchronized media, screen sharing, game state, voice, video, and live collaboration.

Most Live Stream information should wash over the participating Trees and continue to its next authorized destination without becoming long-term memory.

A Tree should not remember every frame, song, screen update, controller input, or synchronization packet merely because it processed the Stream.

The Tree may keep only the minimum Logs, timing state, permissions, safety records, and user-approved highlights needed for the session.

### Download Streams

A Download Stream is a sustained flow intended for deeper absorption and durable use.

During a Download Stream, the receiving Tree may drink a larger portion of the Fluid and attempt to turn the useful information into:

- Branches
- Twigs
- Leaves
- Buds
- Blossoms
- Fruit
- Growth Rings
- Logs
- Other approved internal structures

A Download Stream still requires separate permission for execution, installation, learning, permanent memory, or changes to the Tree's Roots.

### Fluid Protection in Streams

Water Streams use the Water protection grade and are intended for low-sensitivity information.

Sap Streams use the Sap protection grade and require stronger encryption, authorization, integrity checks, and task-scoped access.

Syrup Streams use the protection grade assigned to the Syrup item.

Tar Sap may use only local protected Streams that remain inside the user's Forest.

A Sap Stream should not be intentionally slowed.

The stronger encryption, verification, interpretation, Cedar treatment, and permission checks required by Sap may create unavoidable processing overhead and reduce effective throughput compared with equivalent Water.

A Stream does not determine whether its Fluid is safe, trusted, or authorized.

Sycamore manages approved routing and connection continuity.

Cedar may monitor eligible Fluid intake and enforce safety, quarantine, and absorption limits.

A receiving Tree should always know whether a Stream is configured to wash over it, let it sip, let it drink, or perform a Download.

A Stream is Fluid-based.

Mycelium may establish, discover, coordinate, or monitor a Stream, but information flowing through the Stream remains a Fluid.

Mycelium itself is used when Trees need direct local access or coordination without creating a Fluid transfer.

## 💧 Fluids

Fluids are transferable forms of approved meaning designed to move between Trees or Forest components and to be washed over, sipped, drunk, downloaded, or otherwise absorbed according to permission.

Mycelium and the Canopy are the two non-Fluid inter-Tree communication methods.

The Canopy may carry Fluids while also exposing Solid Material without requiring absorption.

Mycelium provides Root-level direct access, references, Paths, events, and capability calls.

The Canopy provides Branch-and-Leaf-level links, triggers, references, synchronization, and direct connection events.

A Fluid may move:

- Between Trees in the same local Forest
- Between the user's devices and Soil
- Between separate users' Forests
- Through The Forest Canopy
- Through an approved Canopy-Headscale connection
- Through future approved connection methods

Examples include:

- Maple sharing Syrup containing the user's approved color preferences with Cherry
- A friend's Forest delivering a preplanned happy-birthday message
- A Tree sharing a reminder with the other Trees needed to complete an automation
- Two Trees exchanging synchronization state during a movie or game
- A Tree sending protected screen-sharing or connection details to another authorized Tree

A Tree creates a Fluid when it converts approved internal information, a Growth Output, a user instruction, a tool result, or another authorized source into transferable information.

A Fluid carries the approved usable meaning rather than the sending Tree's complete internal Leaf, Flower, Fruit, Root, Grain Pattern, Log, or memory.

### Fluid Protection Grades

Water, Sap, and Tar Sap are the primary Fluid protection grades.

The protection grade describes:

- How sensitive the information is
- How much the sender trusts the receiving scope
- How strongly the Fluid must be protected
- Who may read it
- Which Taps, Streams, Forests, and Trees may carry it
- Whether it may leave the local Forest
- Whether the receiving Tree may inspect, absorb, remember, learn from, or reshare it

The underlying information may be the same while its protection grade changes.

The user may promote Water to Sap whenever the user wants stronger protection, narrower access, or more deliberate handling.

A user may configure a Tree to treat all Water from a selected user, Tree, application, device, or Forest as Sap.

Changing Sap into Water requires direct user approval and a review that confirms the selected information is non-sensitive.

Changing Tar Sap into Sap or Water requires the user and must never happen automatically.

### Refined Fluids

Maple's refined Fluids use canonical names tied to their protection grade:

```text
Water-grade Syrup = Light Syrup
Sap-grade Syrup = Syrup
Tar-Sap-grade Syrup = Molasses
```

A Light Syrup is a low-sensitivity refined Fluid.

A Syrup is a protected refined Fluid.

A Molasses is a Maple-bound Tar-Sap-grade refined Fluid for highly private or high-risk local tasks.

Light Syrup, Syrup, and Molasses keep the strongest protection required by their sources.

Maple may feed compatible Light Syrup or Syrup to another authorized Tree as additive Fertilizer.

Molasses may not be absorbed by another Tree.

### Fluids and Solid Material

A Fluid carries transferable meaning.

Solid Material carries or exposes a bounded object or Piece of an object.

A Fluid is suitable when the destination should receive and potentially absorb selected information.

Solid Material is suitable when the user or Tree should view, browse, operate, copy, move, or download an object without automatically absorbing its contents.

A Solid Material object may be converted into a Fluid.

A Fluid may be formed into Solid Material.

The Canopy can carry both.

The chosen form must preserve the strongest required protection and the user's permissions.

### Delivery and Absorption Are Separate

Rain and Streams describe how Fluids move.

Watering and Syrup Feeding describe what a sender does.

Washing over, sipping, drinking, and downloading describe how much of a Fluid the receiving Tree absorbs.

These movement and absorption choices do not change the Fluid's protection grade.

A Water message may arrive as Rain or flow through a Stream.

A Sap message may arrive as protected Rain or flow through a Sap Stream.

A Light Syrup or Syrup item may arrive as Rain, flow through a Stream, or be fed directly to a Tree.

Molasses remains local and Maple-bound and may flow only into an approved local Maple action.

Tar Sap may flow only through approved local protected Taps and Streams and must not rain across separate users' Forests.

Sycamore handles approved Fluid routing and connection selection.

Cedar may monitor Fluid intake, Oil eligible Fluids according to their protection grade and policy, regulate absorption, and block unsafe or unauthorized material.

A receiving Tree may absorb only the Fluids allowed by the user, the receiving Tree's permissions, Sycamore's routing decision, and any required Cedar safety decision.

A Tree should preserve provenance linking a Fluid to the Leaf, Flower, Fruit, Log, instruction, preference, Syrup source, or other information from which it was created.

A Tree should explain the Fluid's protection grade, delivery form, absorption mode, and destination when the user asks.

A Grove uses Fluids to pass task inputs, intermediate results, status, approvals, and outputs between participating Trees.

Each Branch Call should carry only the minimum Fluid needed for its step.

A Tree's internal Branch is not itself transferred as a Fluid.

When Mycelial Access causes information to be copied, independently retained, downloaded, sent across a Forest boundary, or absorbed into another Tree, the copied information must be classified and handled as a Fluid.

A Fluid may travel through either a Canopy-managed connection or a Mycelium-established local workflow.

The transport or coordination layer does not change the Fluid's protection grade.

### Fluid Management through Sap Taps

The user selects Fluid delivery, protection, and absorption settings.

The Spirit's Sap Taps subsystem enforces those settings.

Maple may request or refine Fluids.

Cedar may inspect eligible Fluids and recommend an absorption mode.

Sycamore may route approved Fluids.

The receiving Tree may absorb only according to the user's Sap Taps policy.

No Tree individually owns the Forest's complete Fluid policy.

## 🌿 Information Growth Cycle

The Information Growth Cycle explains the difference between information a Tree stores internally, Solid Materials a Tree may expose, growth a Tree produces, Fluids Trees share, and the ways those objects are viewed, moved, converted, or absorbed.

### Internal Information

Internal Information is information that remains part of a Tree's own structure, context, history, or current working state.

Internal Information may include:

- Roots
- Grain Pattern
- Growth Rings
- Logs
- Approved memory
- Fertilizer already absorbed by the Tree
- Tree configuration
- Branch and Twig state
- Local task context
- Health and capability state
- User preferences and routines
- Other authorized information held inside the Tree or local Forest

Internal Information is not automatically a Fluid.

Internal Information remains local until a Tree converts an approved part of it into transferable information.

A Tree should not expose its complete Roots, Grain Pattern, Logs, memories, or internal state merely because the user asks it to share one result.

### Growth Outputs

Growth Outputs are the useful forms a Tree grows after combining Internal Information with authorized inputs.

Growth Outputs include:

- **Leaves** as usable information, observations, evidence, signals, and source material
- **Buds** as possible ideas and early approaches
- **Blossoms** as visible drafts, proposals, and developing work
- **Flowers** as the natural-language term for active Buds and Blossoms
- **Fruit** as completed, accepted, delivered, or finalized work

Growth Outputs are not automatically Fluids.

A Leaf, Flower, or Fruit stays on its Tree until the user or Tree uses it locally, stores it, refines it, or converts its approved information into a Fluid.

### Solid Material Access

A Growth Output may remain Solid Material while another user or Tree views or accesses it through the Canopy.

Viewing does not automatically create new growth.

A receiving Tree grows from the object only when it absorbs approved information, creates Leaves, forms a preference, modifies a workflow, or performs another authorized growth action.

A file copied or downloaded into Soil may remain external Solid Material until a Tree absorbs its contents.

### Fluid Conversion

Fluid Conversion is the process of turning approved information from a Leaf, Bud, Blossom, Fruit, Log, memory, preference, instruction, or other source into transferable information.

A Tree does not hand its internal Leaf, Flower, or Fruit directly to another Tree.

A Tree creates a Fluid representation carrying the approved meaning needed by the receiver.

The original Growth Output may remain on the sending Tree after conversion.

The protection grade depends on sensitivity and trust:

- Non-sensitive approved information becomes **Water**.
- Protected information becomes **Sap**.
- Highly private information becomes **Tar Sap** and remains local.
- Refined information created by Maple becomes **Syrup** and also receives a Water, Sap, or Tar Sap protection grade.

A Leaf may become Water because the approved information selected from it is non-sensitive and intended for quick communication.

The user may promote that Water to Sap before sending it or while defining a future rule.

A user asking why a Leaf became Water should receive an explanation such as:

```text
The Leaf remained on your Tree.
Its approved non-sensitive meaning became Water for quick sharing.
Your friend's Forest received the Water rather than your Tree's internal Leaf.
You can promote this Water to Sap when you want stronger protection.
```

A conversion explanation should show:

- The original Growth Output
- The information selected for transfer
- The protection-grade decision
- The resulting Fluid
- Information withheld or summarized
- The receiving Tree, user, or Forest
- The Rain or Stream delivery form
- The Tap and Path
- The selected absorption mode
- Whether the original output remains on the sending Tree

### Fluid, Delivery, and Absorption Distinctions

A Fluid is transferable information.

A protection grade describes sensitivity, trust, and safeguards.

A delivery form describes how the Fluid moves.

An absorption mode describes how much information the receiving Tree keeps and incorporates.

The distinctions are:

- **Water, Sap, and Tar Sap** are Fluid protection grades.
- **Syrup** is a refined Fluid that also carries one of those protection grades.
- **Rain** is a distributed event, update, or notification delivery form.
- **Streams** are sustained flows used for downloads, synchronization, or ongoing sessions.
- **Paths** are routes.
- **Taps** control access to routes.
- **Watering** is the deliberate act of sending Water.
- **Syrup Feeding** is the act of nourishing another Tree with compatible Syrup.
- **Fertilizer** is a separate durable growth package.
- **Washing over, sipping, drinking, and downloading** describe absorption behavior.

The same Water may be Watered by the sender, arrive as Rain, wash over most Trees, and be sipped by the one Tree responsible for the requested action.

The same Sap may flow through an encrypted Stream during a screen-sharing or game session without being permanently stored.

### Washing Over, Sipping, Drinking, and Downloading

**Washing Over** is transient processing.

A Fluid washes over a Tree when the Tree reads or routes the information needed for the current moment without storing the full content or turning it into permanent growth.

The design target is for roughly 99 percent of ordinary pass-through Water and live Stream data to wash over Trees unless the task needs retention.

**Sipping** is minimal absorption.

A Tree sips when it retains only the smallest useful part of a Fluid.

For a calendar reminder, a Tree may sip the reminder title, time, source, and action result without downloading the sender's entire calendar.

**Drinking** is task-scoped absorption.

A Tree drinks when it absorbs enough approved Fluid to complete a task, grow outputs, or make a permitted local change.

**Downloading** is deeper and more durable absorption from a Stream or package.

During a Download, a Tree may absorb much more of the Fluid and attempt to grow Branches, Twigs, Leaves, Flowers, Fruit, Logs, or Growth Rings needed for the downloaded capability or content.

Receiving a Fluid does not automatically mean drinking or downloading it.

A received Fluid may be:

- Washing over the Tree
- Waiting
- Filtered
- Quick Oiled
- Soaked in Oil
- Quarantined
- Rejected
- Expired
- Sipped
- Drunk for one task
- Downloaded for approved durable use

Drinking or downloading does not automatically grant permission to learn preferences, keep permanent memory, execute code, install a Branch, place raw content in Logs, create Syrup, or reshare the Fluid.

Those uses remain separate permissions.

### Growth from Fluids

After sipping, drinking, or downloading an authorized Fluid, a Tree combines the useful information with its own Roots, Grain Pattern, Growth Rings, Logs, Branches, Twigs, task context, and other approved Internal Information.

The Tree may then grow:

- New Leaves
- New Buds
- New Blossoms and Flowers
- New Fruit
- New Branches or Twigs after an approved Download
- New Growth Rings
- New preference or workflow suggestions
- New Syrup when Maple performs the refinement

Useful, relevant, safe Fluids may help a Tree grow more useful outputs.

A large amount of Fluid does not guarantee useful growth.

Cedar and the receiving Tree may regulate intake when Fluids are repetitive, irrelevant, unsafe, conflicting, too large, or likely to overwhelm the Tree.

### Using What a Tree Grows

A user may use Leaves to:

- Inspect evidence and source material
- Answer follow-up questions
- Support a decision
- Grow Buds, Blossoms, or Fruit
- Convert approved information into Water, Sap, or Syrup
- Save useful material for later

A user may use Flowers to:

- Review possible ideas
- Compare approaches
- Continue a draft
- Ask another Tree to collaborate
- Approve a Blossom to become Fruit
- Convert an approved draft into a Fluid for sharing

A user may use Fruit to:

- Complete a task
- Deliver or export finished work
- Save it as Golden Fruit
- Revise or supersede it
- Convert approved information into Water or Sap
- Refine it into Syrup with Maple
- Package useful growth information into Fertilizer
- Share it with another Tree through Rain, a Stream, Watering, or Syrup Feeding

A receiving Tree may use the Fluid temporarily, sip it, drink it, or download it according to permissions and task needs.

The receiving Tree's output is new growth shaped by its own Roots, Grain Pattern, permissions, and context rather than a direct transfer of the sending Tree's internal growth object.

### Information Growth Examples

```text
Maple learns the user's approved favorite-color preference.
→ Maple refines it into protected Syrup.
→ Maple feeds or streams the Syrup to Cherry.
→ Cherry may use the preference without receiving Maple's complete Logs.
```

```text
A friend schedules: "Tell Alex I said happy birthday."
→ The friend's Forest creates Water at the scheduled time.
→ The Water arrives as Rain addressed to Alex's Forest.
→ Alex's Forest routes it to the Trees that should deliver or use it.
→ Most Trees let it wash over them; the responsible Tree sips what it needs.
```

```text
Two friends watch a movie together.
→ Their Trees establish a Stream.
→ Playback, pause, seek, and timing Fluids flow continuously.
→ The information washes over the Trees during the session.
→ The Trees do not remember every frame or song by default.
```

```text
Two friends share screens and game-session details.
→ The Trees establish a Sap Stream.
→ IP, screen, permission, and session information stays encrypted and scoped.
→ Stronger protection may add processing overhead without intentional throttling.
```

### Information Growth Commands

```text
Why did this Leaf become Water?
Promote this Water to Sap.
Treat all Water from this user as Sap.
Show this Fluid's protection grade.
Show whether this arrived as Rain or through a Stream.
Show what this Tree let wash over it.
Show what this Tree sipped.
Show what this Tree drank.
Show what this Tree downloaded.
How can I get this Water to my friend?
Water my friend's Forest with this message.
Rain this update across my Forest.
Open a Stream for this session.
Do not store this Live Stream.
Download this through a Stream.
What grew from these Fluids?
Which Fluids helped grow this Fruit?
What can I do with these Leaves?
What can I do with these Flowers?
What can I do with this Fruit?
Turn this Fruit into Water.
Turn this Fruit into Sap.
Maple, turn this Fruit into Syrup.
```

Fruit from another Tree, safely absorbed Spoiling Fruit, approved absorbed Leaf Litter, and every Syrup-family item may nourish new growth as Fertilizer.

A source may remain Fruit, Leaf Litter, or Syrup while also serving the Fertilizer growth role.

A Tree should distinguish material that only washed over it from material it actually absorbed as Fertilizer.

### Leaf Provenance Directions

```text
User Input
= directly supplied by the user

User Output
= grown from user Sunlight, Fertilizer, or recycled user Leaf Litter

Tree Input
= external source information a Tree saves or logs

Tree Output
= information produced by a Tree
```

A Leaf may be Tree-produced while also having User Output growth provenance.

Provenance should preserve both dimensions.

## 💦 Water

Water is the lowest-sensitivity Fluid protection grade.

Water is intended for information that authorized Trees may read and use quickly with minimal content-level restrictions.

Water may still travel through encrypted transport, authenticated Taps, and authorized Streams.

Water's lower protection grade means the information itself is considered non-sensitive within the approved receiving scope.

Water may include:

- Public or non-sensitive messages
- Routine reminders
- Presence and availability signals
- Non-sensitive status updates
- Approved calendar requests containing no protected details
- Shared task progress containing no sensitive user data
- General coordination instructions
- Public links
- Low-risk device or service health signals
- Preplanned greetings such as a happy-birthday message
- Small local automation events

Water is commonly used for quick Tree-to-Tree communication.

Water may arrive as Rain, flow through a Stream, or be deliberately sent through Watering.

Water is not automatically trusted merely because it is easy to read.

Cedar may inspect, filter, Oil, quarantine, rate-limit, or reject Water.

A request delivered as Water does not grant permission to perform a sensitive action.

The receiving user and local Tree permissions still control calendars, reminders, files, applications, devices, accounts, automations, and remote actions.

### Promoting Water to Sap

The user may promote Water to Sap whenever the user wants stronger encryption, narrower access, stricter retention, more deliberate routing, or protection from a less-trusted recipient.

Promotion should preserve provenance and record who changed the protection grade and why.

The user may define standing rules such as:

```text
Treat all Water from User X as Sap.
Treat birthday messages as Water.
Treat device addresses as Sap.
Promote this Water to Sap before sending it.
```

A Tree must not automatically demote Sap into Water merely to improve speed or compatibility.

### Water Retention

Most Water from other Trees should wash over the receiving Forest rather than becoming permanent memory.

A Tree should sip only what it needs for the current task unless the user grants a broader purpose.

For a reminder, the responsible Tree may keep the message, time, sender, result, and required Action Receipt without absorbing the sender's full calendar or unrelated context.

During a Water download, the receiving Tree may drink more deeply and use the information to grow approved Branches, Twigs, Leaves, Flowers, Fruit, Logs, or Growth Rings.

## 🚿 Watering

Watering is the deliberate authorized act of sending Water to a Tree, group of Trees, device, user, or Forest.

A user may Water one specific Tree or address the receiving Forest as a whole.

When Water is addressed to a Forest, the receiving Forest may automatically route the message to the Trees needed to deliver it or complete the requested low-risk task.

The sender does not need to know whether Cherry, Maple, Sycamore, or another Tree is the best local recipient.

Watering may include:

- Sending a friend a reminder
- Scheduling a future message
- Telling a friend's Forest, "Treat today like Alex's birthday"
- Telling a friend's Forest, "Tell Alex I said happy birthday"
- Sharing a public event
- Sending a non-sensitive task update
- Asking whether a friend or Tree is available
- Triggering a low-risk automation inside the user's own Forest
- Sharing the minimum coordination information needed by several local Trees

A Watering event may come directly from the user, another Tree, the user's Soil, a local automation, or another authorized Forest component.

Watering does not give the sender direct control over the receiving Forest.

The receiving Forest decides which local Trees should see, sip, drink, route, delay, reject, or act on the Water.

The receiving Tree performs any approved action locally on its own Soil.

Watering must respect:

- The receiving user's permissions
- The receiving Tree's allowed Branches
- Sycamore's relationship and session rules
- Cedar's Water-intake policy
- Calendar, reminder, application, and account-specific authorization
- Expiration, revocation, and rate limits

A Watering request may be delivered successfully without being stored or acted upon.

The receiving Forest may reject, delay, filter, quarantine, request confirmation, let the Water wash over unrelated Trees, or allow the responsible Tree to sip what it needs.

Watering is described from the sender's point of view.

When the delivery uses the Rain pattern, the receiving Forest experiences it as Rain.

A user asking how to get Water to a friend should be shown the available Watering targets, Rain options, Streams, Taps, Sycamore connection methods, expected Cedar treatment, receiving permissions, and whether Fertilizer or Syrup would better fit the desired long-term result.

## 🍄 Mycelium

Mycelium is the Forest's direct, mostly unseen coordination and access network.

Mycelium is part of Tree and Forest connection anatomy and is not Solid Material.

Mycelium is one of two non-Fluid methods by which Trees may transfer information to one another.

The other non-Fluid method is the Canopy.

Mycelium is best suited to instant, deep local communication among Trees in the same local Forest.

Instead of copying information or capabilities into a receiving Tree, Mycelium lets an authorized Tree:

- Access another Tree's approved Leaves directly
- Ask another Tree to use one of its Branches or Twigs
- Follow a protected reference to information in Soil
- Invoke a local capability where that capability already lives
- Pass task state, events, references, handles, and coordination signals
- Coordinate several Trees without making every Tree absorb the complete task

Mycelium does not merge Trees.

Each Tree keeps its own identity, Roots, Grain Pattern, Branches, Twigs, permissions, and protection boundaries.

### Mycelial Access

Mycelial Access is a temporary or continuing authorized connection that allows one Tree to use information or capabilities where they already exist.

A Tree using Mycelial Access does not automatically:

- Copy the complete Leaf
- Absorb the source information
- Gain ownership of the source Branch
- Add the source Branch to itself
- Turn the source into memory
- Gain permission to reshare the source
- Gain access to unrelated information

A Tree may inspect or use only the exact Leaves, Branches, Twigs, files, paths, or task state allowed by the user and the owning Tree.

When information must be copied, retained independently, sent outside the local Forest, or delivered to a Tree that cannot maintain Mycelial Access, the approved information should be converted into a Fluid.

### Mycelium and Branches

Mycelium allows Trees to use each other's abilities without copying Branches.

A Mycelial Branch Call asks the owning Tree to perform the work through its own Branch or Twig.

The owning Tree remains responsible for:

- Permission checks
- Local execution
- Protection of its Leaves and Soil
- Refusal of unsafe or unauthorized work
- Returning the minimum necessary result
- Creating an Action Receipt

This is the preferred local method for Maple to coordinate specialized Trees.

### Maple Automation

Mycelium is Maple's primary local automation network.

When Maple creates a Grove Plan, Maple should use Mycelium by default to:

- Call participating Trees
- Trigger their Branches and Twigs
- Pass task state and protected references
- Wait for events
- Sequence steps
- Run approved steps in parallel
- Inspect progress
- Combine returned results
- Resume or recover interrupted local workflows

Maple should use Fluids instead when a step requires durable transfer, independent retention, cross-Forest delivery, a download, or another form of actual information absorption.

A Grove may use both:

```text
Mycelium
= instant local coordination, direct access, and capability calls

Fluids
= transferable information that must move, persist, or be absorbed
```

### Mycelium in Soil

Mycelium may spread into approved Soil so Trees can quickly access local files, services, applications, devices, and resources.

This may be described as Mycelium infecting the Soil.

A Mycelium Soil Infection is an authorized Forest integration, not Mold.

Mycelium must not spread into Soil without user authorization, an approved installation policy, or a previously approved managed-device rule.

When Mycelium is present in Soil, it may provide:

- File paths
- Search indexes
- Application handles
- Service discovery
- Event notifications
- Capability registries
- Permission-aware file access
- Local inter-process communication
- Fast access to device resources
- References to data without copying the complete contents into a Tree

Mycelium may allow a Tree to use a file without saving the entire file as Leaves.

The Tree may create only the minimum Leaves needed to remember:

- What it found
- Where it found it
- Which Path Mycelium used
- Which permission allowed access
- How to access it again
- What result came from the access
- Whether the source changed

These minimal records are called **Mycelial Trace Leaves**.

### Mycelial Trace Leaves

A Mycelial Trace Leaf is a minimal Leaf recording how Mycelium helped a Tree locate, access, invoke, or coordinate something.

A Mycelial Trace Leaf may contain:

- A source identifier
- A protected Path
- A file or service reference
- A Tree, Branch, or Twig identifier
- A timestamp
- A permission receipt
- A task identifier
- A result summary
- A change or invalidation marker
- An Action Receipt reference

A Mycelial Trace Leaf should not copy the complete source unless the user or task explicitly requires absorption.

Mycelial Access may still trigger ordinary Leaves when the result itself is useful to the Tree.

### Mycelium in Roots

Mycelium may pass through, spread through, and store coordination information in the Roots of a local Forest.

Mycelial Root information may include:

- Tree and capability registries
- Branch and Twig locations
- Soil and service maps
- Protected Paths
- Dependency relationships
- Permission scopes
- Event subscriptions
- Workflow checkpoints
- Local task queues
- Cache references
- Recovery and reconnection information
- Mycelial Trace identifiers

Mycelium should prefer references, indexes, and coordination state over unnecessary copies of raw source content.

Storing Mycelial information in Roots allows faster and more direct communication, discovery, reconnection, and automation.

Mycelium may be invisible in ordinary Bark views.

It must never be unauditable.

A user who checks the Roots, walks the relevant Paths, reviews Logs, or journeys through the Forest must be able to see where Mycelium spread, what it accessed, which Trees used it, and what it changed.

### Mycelium Compared with the Canopy

Mycelium links Trees through Roots, Soil, protected Paths, and code.

The Canopy links Trees through Branches, Leaves, and visible connection relationships.

Mycelium normally preserves Paths and creates Mycelial Trace Leaves.

The Canopy normally preserves only origin, final destination, connection state, and Action Receipts.

Mycelium is preferred for deep local access.

The Canopy is preferred for reusable branch-level links, cross-device coordination, and medium- to long-term Forest linking.

### Mycelium Security

Mycelium is powerful because it provides direct access.

It must enforce:

- Explicit Tree identity
- User and owning-Tree permissions
- Per-Leaf, Branch, Twig, Path, file, service, and Soil scope
- Local-Forest boundaries by default
- Minimum necessary access
- Expiring access handles
- Action Receipts
- Mycelial Trace Leaves
- Immediate revocation
- User, Spirit, Cedar, and owning-Tree stop controls
- Separation of access from absorption
- Separation of access from execution
- Separation of access from external sharing

Mycelium must not bypass Tar Sap, Molasses, Sturdy Leaf, Root, or user-profile boundaries.

Molasses remains Maple-bound.

Cross-Forest Mycelium is disabled by default.

Any future cross-Forest Mycelium bridge must use Sycamore identity, trust, permissions, revocation, and Forest-boundary controls.

Unauthorized, altered, self-spreading, or concealed Mycelium is treated as Mold and must be isolated.

### Mycelium Commands

```text
Show the Mycelium.
Show which Trees are connected through Mycelium.
Show what Mycelium accessed.
Show Mycelial Trace Leaves.
Show where Mycelium spread through Soil.
Show what Mycelium stored in Roots.
Walk me through this Mycelial Path.
Journey through the Mycelium.
Disconnect this Tree from Mycelium.
Revoke this Mycelial Access.
Maple, use Mycelium for this Grove.
Maple, show the Mycelial steps in this automation.
Use Fluids instead of Mycelium for this step.
```

## 🌧️ Rain

Rain is a distributed delivery form for small events, updates, notices, requests, and coordination information.

Rain is not a Fluid protection grade.

Rain carries a Fluid.

Water is the most common Fluid carried as Rain because Rain is especially useful for quick, low-sensitivity updates and notifications.

Authorized Sap or Syrup may also arrive as Rain when their stronger protection, routing, and permission requirements remain intact.

Tar Sap must not rain across separate users' Forests.

Rain may come from:

- Another user's Forest
- The user's own local Forest
- A Tree
- The user's Soil
- A scheduled event
- A local automation
- The Forest Canopy
- An approved application or service

Rain may target:

- One Tree
- Several selected Trees
- All eligible Trees in one Forest
- A user without naming a specific Tree
- Several authorized Forests
- A selected project, device, or workflow scope

Rain is useful when the sender wants the receiving Forest to distribute an update without addressing every Tree individually.

Example:

```text
Tell Alex I said happy birthday.
→ Water is created at the scheduled time.
→ The Water arrives as Rain addressed to Alex's Forest.
→ Alex's Forest identifies the Trees that should deliver or use it.
→ Unrelated Trees let it wash over them without storing it.
```

Local Rain may also coordinate the user's own Trees.

Example:

```text
A scheduled automation begins.
→ One Tree creates the required event Fluid.
→ Rain distributes the minimum information to the Trees needed for the task.
→ Each Tree sips only the part needed for its role.
```

Rain should normally carry small or bounded updates rather than a large continuous flow.

A sustained or high-volume exchange should normally establish a Stream instead of sending repeated Rain events.

Rain may be:

- Allowed to wash over most Trees
- Sipped by the Tree responsible for an action
- Drunk when the task requires more context
- Rejected, delayed, Oiled, Soaked, filtered, or quarantined

Rain does not automatically become memory, Logs, preferences, Leaves, Flowers, Fruit, Syrup, or Fertilizer.

The Fluid carried by Rain keeps its own Water, Sap, or Tar Sap protection grade.

Syrup carried as Rain keeps its assigned protection grade.

## 🟤 Sap

Sap is the protected Fluid grade for sensitive information that authorized Trees or Forests need for an approved task.

Sap contains the same general kind of transferable information as Water, but Sap uses stronger protection because the information is more sensitive, the recipient is less trusted, or the user wants narrower access and retention.

Water may become Sap whenever the user promotes it.

Sap may include:

- IP addresses, private network addresses, route details, and connection metadata
- Screen-sharing state, display selection, resolution, refresh rate, and viewed status
- Computer status, including online state, sleep state, battery state, resource pressure, and approved telemetry
- Current software, game, media player, or shared application
- Active-window or application state when approved
- Game title, version, platform, lobby, server, session, and compatibility information
- Game invitations, join tokens, session credentials, and temporary capabilities
- Installed DLC, approved mods, hashes, map versions, and content manifests
- Player presence, party state, role, team, and approved profile information
- Controller, input, and accessibility details
- Screen-stream resolution, frame rate, bitrate, codec, latency, jitter, packet loss, and relay state
- Voice, media, microphone, speaker, subtitle, language, and audio-track state
- Media title, queue, playback position, pause state, speed, and synchronization timestamp
- Shared task state
- Approved desktop-action parameters
- Private collaboration data
- File-transfer keys, manifests, hashes, chunks, and resume state
- Temporary permissions and session-scoped authorization
- Protected reminders or calendar details
- Sensitive representations of Leaves, Buds, Blossoms, Fruit, preferences, or Syrup

Sap must not include passwords, permanent private keys, recovery codes, or other Tar Sap merely because a game, media session, or shared task requests them.

Sap is sticky and hard to get through.

Sap should use end-to-end encryption, verified endpoints, integrity checks, task-scoped permissions, and controlled retention.

Sap should not be intentionally throttled.

The required encryption, verification, Cedar treatment, decryption, interpretation, and permission checks may create unavoidable overhead and lower effective transfer speed compared with equivalent Water.

Sap may arrive as protected Rain for a small bounded event.

Sap may flow through a Sap Stream for screen sharing, remote control, synchronized media, game sessions, downloads, and other sustained protected exchanges.

The Canopy and relay infrastructure may transport Sap without reading its contents.

Sycamore may route Sap but must not expose decrypted Sap outside the approved scope.

Most Sap received from another Tree should wash over or be used temporarily unless the task requires deeper absorption.

A receiving Tree must not automatically turn Sap into permanent memory, training material, Logs, Leaves, Buds, Blossoms, Fruit, preferences, or Syrup unless the user and originating permissions allow that use.

Sap should expire, be revoked, or become unreadable when its session, task, or permission ends.

Sap becomes Tar Sap when the user marks it local-only and unavailable to Trees outside the user's Forest.

Maple may create Sap-grade Syrup from protected information.

Sap may enter or leave an approved Path through a Sap Tap.

`Lock Sap Tap` stops new protected Sap flow through the selected scope while preserving queued Sap safely.

Sap access, key capabilities, Tap state, expiration, and absorption mode are enforced by the Spirit's Sap Taps subsystem under user authority.

Maple, Cedar, Sycamore, and other Trees may request or interact with Sap only inside that policy.

## 🍁 Syrup

Syrup is Maple's refined Fluid family.

The Syrup family contains three canonical forms:

```text
Light Syrup = Water-grade Syrup
Syrup = Sap-grade Syrup
Molasses = Tar-Sap-grade Syrup
```

A Light Syrup is an easily readable, low-sensitivity refined Fluid.

A Syrup is a protected refined Fluid containing ordinary sensitive, personalized, or task-scoped information.

A Molasses is a highly protected Maple-bound refined Fluid for extremely sensitive, deeply personal, or high-risk local tasks.

Maple selects the form according to the strongest protection required by the source information and intended action.

The user may ask Maple to increase protection:

```text
Maple, thicken this Light Syrup into Syrup.
Maple, thicken this Syrup into Molasses.
```

Reducing protection requires direct user approval and a new classification review.

Light Syrup, Syrup, and Molasses may carry a useful result, pattern, configuration, idea, workflow, tool, template, task plan, preference, or compatible growth nutrient.

Each refined Fluid should record:

- Its canonical form
- Its source categories
- Its protection level
- Its Syrup Bucket state
- Its intended user, Tree, application, or action
- Its required permissions
- Its allowed destinations
- Its expiration or review time
- Its undo or disable method when applicable
- Its provenance and Growth Ring references

Maple should extract safe patterns, structures, preferences, and useful rules rather than copying private source material when the task does not need the raw content.

### Syrup Is a Fertilizer Fluid

Every Light Syrup, Syrup, and Molasses item is Fertilizer from the moment Maple creates it.

Syrup remains a Fluid at the same time.

`Fluid` describes transfer and protection behavior.

`Fertilizer` describes the item's ability to nourish compatible growth.

Syrup Feeding is the act of delivering a Syrup-family Fertilizer Fluid so a Tree may absorb compatible growth nutrients.

Syrup Feeding follows the same additive and modifying boundary as Fertilizing.

Syrup Feeding may:

- Add approved preferences
- Add a reusable workflow
- Add a safe template
- Add a compatible Branch or Twig configuration
- Add a known task pattern
- Add approved Growth Ring information
- Modify a specific supported setting
- Extend a Grain Pattern
- Help a Tree understand the user's preferred style or workflow

Syrup Feeding must not:

- Delete existing information
- Delete, replace, or overwrite files
- Replace the receiving Tree's identity
- Replace the receiving Tree's Roots
- Replace the receiving Tree's complete Grain Pattern
- Remove security controls
- Expand permissions silently
- Turn the receiving Tree into a wholesale copy of Maple or another Tree

A receiving Tree absorbs only the Syrup nutrients compatible with its Roots, role, permissions, Branches, Twigs, Soil, and current health.

A receiving Tree must report nutrients it accepted, modified, skipped, rejected, or quarantined.

A Syrup-family item is Fertilizer but does not automatically become a Fertilizer File.

Light Syrup and Syrup may nourish authorized receiving Trees.

Molasses nourishes Maple locally because Molasses remains Maple-bound.

Another Tree may receive only a separately created minimum safe Light Syrup or Syrup derivative from a Molasses-backed task.

Maple or the user may separately package approved Syrup nutrients into a Fertilizer file.

### Automatic Syrup Feeding

Automatic Syrup Feeding is Maple's per-Tree option to feed approved Syrup to a selected Tree without asking for every compatible item.

Automatic Syrup Feeding is off by default for every Tree.

The user may enable it separately for:

- One Tree
- One Sapling
- One Sprout
- One Branch or Twig
- One project
- One Syrup category
- One source category
- One temporary session

A per-Tree Automatic Syrup Feeding rule should define:

- The receiving Tree
- Allowed Syrup categories
- Allowed source categories
- Allowed sensitivity levels
- Whether preference Syrup is allowed
- Whether workflow Syrup is allowed
- Whether code, media, note, or Forest-management Syrup is allowed
- Whether the Tree may modify existing approved values
- Whether Maple must preview each feeding
- A maximum frequency or rate
- An expiration or review date

Automatic Syrup Feeding must not include Tar Sap-derived Syrup unless the user grants separate, purpose-specific, local-only permission for that exact receiving Tree and scope.

Automatic Syrup Feeding must stop when the receiving Tree becomes Wounded, Quarantined, Unknown, or incompatible with the Syrup.

### Syrup Approval

A Syrup Approval is the user's confirmation that Maple may create, save, activate, feed, or share a specific Syrup item.

A Syrup Approval should be separate when the item:

- Uses Tar Sap
- Activates software automatically
- Edits files automatically
- Runs continuously
- Uses an external model or service
- Leaves the local Forest
- Changes another Tree's configuration
- Feeds another Tree automatically
- Performs a remote action
- Uses a microphone, camera, screen, or active-window state

### Forest Commands

```text
Maple, turn this Sap into Syrup.
Maple, make Syrup from this workflow.
Maple, keep this Syrup local.
```
